					<div id="mw-content-text" class="mw-body-content mw-content-ltr" lang="en" dir="ltr"><div class="mw-parser-output"><div class="shortdescription nomobile noexcerpt noprint searchaux" style="display:none">Head of the Catholic Church from 1523 to 1534</div>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Not to be confused with <a href="/wiki/Antipope_Clement_VII" title="Antipope Clement VII">Antipope Clement VII</a>.</div>
<p class="mw-empty-elt">
</p>

<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1066479718"><table class="infobox vcard"><tbody><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-above" style="font-weight: normal; background-color: gold;"><span style="font-size:80%">Papal styles of</span><br /><b><span class="fn">Pope Clement VII</span></b></th></tr><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-image"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:Medici_popes.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6b/Medici_popes.svg/60px-Medici_popes.svg.png" decoding="async" width="60" height="71" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6b/Medici_popes.svg/90px-Medici_popes.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6b/Medici_popes.svg/120px-Medici_popes.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1275" data-file-height="1518" /></a></span></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Style_(manner_of_address)" class="mw-redirect" title="Style (manner of address)">Reference style</a></th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/His_Holiness" class="mw-redirect" title="His Holiness">His Holiness</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Spoken style</th><td class="infobox-data">Your Holiness</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Religious style</th><td class="infobox-data">Holy Father</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Posthumous style</th><td class="infobox-data">None</td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Pope Clement VII</b> (<a href="/wiki/Latin_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Latin language">Latin</a>: <i lang="la">Clemens VII</i>; <a href="/wiki/Italian_language" title="Italian language">Italian</a>: <i lang="it">Clemente VII</i>; born <b>Giulio de' Medici</b>; 26 May 1478 – 25 September 1534) was head of the <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church" title="Catholic Church">Catholic Church</a> and ruler of the <a href="/wiki/Papal_States" title="Papal States">Papal States</a> from 19 November 1523 to his death, on 25 September 1534. Deemed "the most unfortunate of the popes", Clement VII's reign was marked by a rapid succession of political, military, and religious struggles—many long in the making—which had far-reaching consequences for Christianity and world politics.<sup id="cite_ref-Clement_VII_3-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Clement_VII-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Elected in 1523 at the end of the <a href="/wiki/Italian_Renaissance" title="Italian Renaissance">Italian Renaissance</a>, Clement came to the papacy with a high reputation as a statesman.<sup id="cite_ref-encyclopedia.com_4-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-encyclopedia.com-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> He had served with distinction as chief advisor to <a href="/wiki/Pope_Leo_X" title="Pope Leo X">Pope Leo X</a> (1513–1521, his cousin), <a href="/wiki/Pope_Adrian_VI" title="Pope Adrian VI">Pope Adrian VI</a> (1522–1523), and commendably as <a href="/wiki/Gran_maestro" title="Gran maestro">gran maestro</a> of <a href="/wiki/Florence,_Italy" class="mw-redirect" title="Florence, Italy">Florence</a> (1519–1523).<sup id="cite_ref-oll.libertyfund.org_5-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-oll.libertyfund.org-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-encyclopedia.com_4-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-encyclopedia.com-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> Assuming leadership at a time of crisis, with the <a href="/wiki/Protestant_Reformation" class="mw-redirect" title="Protestant Reformation">Protestant Reformation</a> spreading; the Church nearing bankruptcy; and large, <a href="/wiki/Italian_Wars" title="Italian Wars">foreign armies invading Italy</a>, Clement initially tried to unite Christendom by making peace among the many <a href="/wiki/Christian" class="mw-redirect" title="Christian">Christian</a> leaders then at odds.<sup id="cite_ref-Ashgate_7-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ashgate-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> He later attempted to liberate Italy from foreign occupation, believing that it threatened the Church's freedom.<sup id="cite_ref-Clement_VII_3-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Clement_VII-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The complex political situation of the 1520s thwarted Clement's efforts.<sup id="cite_ref-newadvent.org_8-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-newadvent.org-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> Inheriting unprecedented challenges, including <a href="/wiki/Martin_Luther" title="Martin Luther">Martin Luther</a>'s <a href="/wiki/Protestant_Reformation" class="mw-redirect" title="Protestant Reformation">Protestant Reformation</a> in Northern Europe; a vast power struggle in Italy between Europe's two most powerful kings, <a href="/wiki/Holy_Roman_Emperor_Charles_V" class="mw-redirect" title="Holy Roman Emperor Charles V">Holy Roman Emperor Charles V</a> and <a href="/wiki/Francis_I_of_France" title="Francis I of France">Francis I of France</a>, each of whom demanded that the Pope choose a side; and <a href="/wiki/Turkish_people" title="Turkish people">Turkish</a> invasions of Eastern Europe led by <a href="/wiki/Suleiman_the_Magnificent" title="Suleiman the Magnificent">Suleiman the Magnificent</a>, Clement's problems were exacerbated by <a href="/wiki/King_Henry_VIII_of_England" class="mw-redirect" title="King Henry VIII of England">King Henry VIII of England</a>'s contentious divorce, resulting in <a href="/wiki/English_Reformation" title="English Reformation">England breaking away from the Catholic Church</a>; and in 1527, souring relations with Emperor Charles V, leading to the violent <a href="/wiki/Sack_of_Rome_(1527)" title="Sack of Rome (1527)">Sack of Rome</a>, during which Clement was imprisoned. After escaping confinement in the <a href="/wiki/Castel_Sant%27Angelo" title="Castel Sant&#39;Angelo">Castel Sant'Angelo</a>, Clement—with few economic, military, or political options remaining—compromised the Church's and Italy's independence by allying with his former jailer, Charles V.<sup id="cite_ref-Clement_VII_3-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Clement_VII-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-encyclopedia.com_4-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-encyclopedia.com-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In contrast to his tortured pontificate, Clement was personally respectable and devout, possessing a "dignified propriety of character", "great acquirements both theological and scientific", as well as "extraordinary address and penetration—Clement VII, in serener times, might have administered the Papal power with high reputation and enviable prosperity. But with all of his profound insight into the political affairs of Europe, Clement does not seem to have comprehended the altered position of the Pope" in relation to Europe's emerging nation-states and Protestantism.<sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Clement left a significant cultural legacy in the Medici tradition.<sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> He commissioned artworks by <a href="/wiki/Raphael" title="Raphael">Raphael</a>, <a href="/wiki/Benvenuto_Cellini" title="Benvenuto Cellini">Benvenuto Cellini</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Michelangelo" title="Michelangelo">Michelangelo</a>, including Michelangelo's <i><a href="/wiki/The_Last_Judgment_(Michelangelo)" title="The Last Judgment (Michelangelo)">The Last Judgment</a></i> in the <a href="/wiki/Sistine_Chapel" title="Sistine Chapel">Sistine Chapel</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-auto4_12-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto4-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> In matters of science, Clement is best known for approving, in 1533, <a href="/wiki/Nicolaus_Copernicus" title="Nicolaus Copernicus">Nicolaus Copernicus</a>'s theory that the Earth revolves around the Sun—99 years before <a href="/wiki/Galileo_Galilei" title="Galileo Galilei">Galileo Galilei</a>'s heresy trial for similar ideas.<sup id="cite_ref-auto1_14-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto1-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-auto6_15-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto6-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-auto_16-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life">Early life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Clement_VII&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Early life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Leonardo_da_Vinci_-_Hanging_of_Bernardo_Baroncelli_1479.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2f/Leonardo_da_Vinci_-_Hanging_of_Bernardo_Baroncelli_1479.jpg/220px-Leonardo_da_Vinci_-_Hanging_of_Bernardo_Baroncelli_1479.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="574" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2f/Leonardo_da_Vinci_-_Hanging_of_Bernardo_Baroncelli_1479.jpg/330px-Leonardo_da_Vinci_-_Hanging_of_Bernardo_Baroncelli_1479.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2f/Leonardo_da_Vinci_-_Hanging_of_Bernardo_Baroncelli_1479.jpg/440px-Leonardo_da_Vinci_-_Hanging_of_Bernardo_Baroncelli_1479.jpg 2x" data-file-width="766" data-file-height="2000" /></a><figcaption>Hanging of Bernardo Baroncelli, <a href="/wiki/Leonardo_da_Vinci" title="Leonardo da Vinci">Leonardo da Vinci</a>, 1479. Pazzi Conspirator.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Giulio de' Medici's life began under tragic circumstances. On 26 April 1478—exactly one month before his birth—his father, <a href="/wiki/Giuliano_de_Medici" class="mw-redirect" title="Giuliano de Medici">Giuliano de Medici</a> (brother of <a href="/wiki/Lorenzo_the_Magnificent" class="mw-redirect" title="Lorenzo the Magnificent">Lorenzo the Magnificent</a>) was <a href="/wiki/Pazzi_Conspiracy" class="mw-redirect" title="Pazzi Conspiracy">murdered</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Florence_Cathedral" title="Florence Cathedral">Florence Cathedral</a> by enemies of his family, in what is now known as "<a href="/wiki/Pazzi_conspiracy" title="Pazzi conspiracy">The Pazzi Conspiracy</a>".<sup id="cite_ref-fiu_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-fiu-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> He was born illegitimately on 26 May 1478, in Florence; the exact identity of his mother remains unknown, although a plurality of scholars contend that it was <a href="/wiki/Fioretta_Gorini" title="Fioretta Gorini">Fioretta Gorini</a>, the daughter of a professor, Antonio Gorini.<sup id="cite_ref-fiu_17-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-fiu-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-catholic_18-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-catholic-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> Giulio spent the first seven years of life with his godfather, the architect <a href="/wiki/Antonio_da_Sangallo_the_Elder" title="Antonio da Sangallo the Elder">Antonio da Sangallo the Elder</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-fiu_17-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-fiu-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Thereafter, Lorenzo the Magnificent raised him as one of his own sons, alongside his children <a href="/wiki/Pope_Leo_X" title="Pope Leo X">Giovanni</a> (the future <a href="/wiki/Pope_Leo_X" title="Pope Leo X">Pope Leo X</a>), <a href="/wiki/Piero_the_Unfortunate" title="Piero the Unfortunate">Piero</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Giuliano_de%27_Medici,_Duke_of_Nemours" title="Giuliano de&#39; Medici, Duke of Nemours">Giuliano</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-paradox_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-paradox-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> Educated at the <a href="/wiki/Palazzo_Medici" class="mw-redirect" title="Palazzo Medici">Palazzo Medici</a> in Florence by humanists like <a href="/wiki/Angelo_Poliziano" class="mw-redirect" title="Angelo Poliziano">Angelo Poliziano</a>, and alongside prodigies like <a href="/wiki/Michelangelo" title="Michelangelo">Michelangelo</a>, Giulio became an accomplished musician.<sup id="cite_ref-paradox_19-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-paradox-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> In personality he was reputed to be shy, and in physical appearance, handsome.<sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Giulio's natural inclination was for the clergy, but his illegitimacy barred him from high-ranking positions in the Church. So Lorenzo the Magnificent helped him carve out a career as a soldier.<sup id="cite_ref-fiu_17-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-fiu-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> He was enrolled in the <a href="/wiki/Knights_Hospitaller" title="Knights Hospitaller">Knights of Rhodes</a>, but also became <a href="/wiki/Grand_Prior" class="mw-redirect" title="Grand Prior">Grand Prior</a> of <a href="/wiki/Capua" title="Capua">Capua</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-fiu_17-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-fiu-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> In 1492, when Lorenzo the Magnificent died and Giovanni de' Medici assumed his duties as a cardinal, Giulio became more involved in Church affairs.<sup id="cite_ref-fiu_17-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-fiu-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> He studied canon law at the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Pisa" title="University of Pisa">University of Pisa</a>, and accompanied Giovanni to the <a href="/wiki/Conclave_of_1492" class="mw-redirect" title="Conclave of 1492">conclave of 1492</a>, where <a href="/wiki/Rodrigo_Borgia" class="mw-redirect" title="Rodrigo Borgia">Rodrigo Borgia</a> was elected <a href="/wiki/Pope_Alexander_VI" title="Pope Alexander VI">Pope Alexander VI</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-fiu_17-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-fiu-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Following the misfortunes of Lorenzo the Magnificent's firstborn son, <a href="/wiki/Piero_the_Unfortunate" title="Piero the Unfortunate">Piero the Unfortunate</a>, the Medici were expelled from Florence in 1494.<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> Over the next six years, Cardinal Giovanni and Giulio wandered throughout Europe together—twice getting arrested (first in <a href="/wiki/Ulm,_Germany" class="mw-redirect" title="Ulm, Germany">Ulm, Germany</a>, and later in <a href="/wiki/Rouen,_France" class="mw-redirect" title="Rouen, France">Rouen, France</a>). Each time Piero the Unfortunate bailed them out.<sup id="cite_ref-fiu_17-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-fiu-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> In 1500, both returned to Italy and concentrated their efforts on re-establishing their family in Florence. Both were present at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Ravenna_(1512)" title="Battle of Ravenna (1512)">Battle of Ravenna</a> in 1512, where Cardinal Giovanni was captured by the French but Giulio escaped; this led to Giulio becoming an emissary to <a href="/wiki/Pope_Julius_II" title="Pope Julius II">Pope Julius II</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-auto7_23-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto7-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> That same year, with the assistance of Pope Julius and the Spanish troops of <a href="/wiki/Ferdinand_II_of_Aragon" title="Ferdinand II of Aragon">Ferdinand of Aragon</a>, the Medici retook control of Florence.<sup id="cite_ref-fiu_17-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-fiu-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Paternity_of_Alessandro_de.27_Medici"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Paternity_of_Alessandro_de'_Medici">Paternity of Alessandro de' Medici</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Clement_VII&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Paternity of Alessandro de&#039; Medici">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In 1510, while the Medici were living near Rome, a servant in their household—identified in documents as <a href="/w/index.php?title=Simonetta_da_Collevecchio&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Simonetta da Collevecchio (page does not exist)">Simonetta da Collevecchio</a><span class="noprint" style="font-size:85%; font-style: normal;">&#160;&#91;<a href="https://it.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simonetta_da_Collevecchio" class="extiw" title="it:Simonetta da Collevecchio">it</a>&#93;</span>—became pregnant, ultimately giving birth to a son, <a href="/wiki/Alessandro_de%27_Medici" class="mw-redirect" title="Alessandro de&#39; Medici">Alessandro de' Medici</a>. Nicknamed "il Moro" ("the Moor") due to his dark complexion, Alessandro was officially recognized as the illegitimate son of <a href="/wiki/Lorenzo_II_de_Medici" class="mw-redirect" title="Lorenzo II de Medici">Lorenzo II de Medici</a>, but at the time and to this day, various scholars suggest that Alessandro was the illegitimate son of Giulio de' Medici.<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> The truth of his lineage remains unknown and debated.<sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Regardless of his paternity, throughout Alessandro's brief life, Giulio—as Pope Clement VII—showed him great favoritism, elevating Alessandro over <a href="/wiki/Ippolito_de_Medici" class="mw-redirect" title="Ippolito de Medici">Ippolito de Medici</a> as Florence's first hereditary monarch, despite the latter's comparable qualifications.<sup id="cite_ref-pbs_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-pbs-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Cardinal">Cardinal</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Clement_VII&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Cardinal">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Under_Pope_Leo_X">Under Pope Leo X</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Clement_VII&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Under Pope Leo X">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Portrait_of_Pope_Leo_X_and_his_cousins,_cardinals_Giulio_de%27_Medici_and_Luigi_de%27_Rossi_(by_Raphael).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5a/Portrait_of_Pope_Leo_X_and_his_cousins%2C_cardinals_Giulio_de%27_Medici_and_Luigi_de%27_Rossi_%28by_Raphael%29.jpg/220px-Portrait_of_Pope_Leo_X_and_his_cousins%2C_cardinals_Giulio_de%27_Medici_and_Luigi_de%27_Rossi_%28by_Raphael%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="291" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5a/Portrait_of_Pope_Leo_X_and_his_cousins%2C_cardinals_Giulio_de%27_Medici_and_Luigi_de%27_Rossi_%28by_Raphael%29.jpg/330px-Portrait_of_Pope_Leo_X_and_his_cousins%2C_cardinals_Giulio_de%27_Medici_and_Luigi_de%27_Rossi_%28by_Raphael%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5a/Portrait_of_Pope_Leo_X_and_his_cousins%2C_cardinals_Giulio_de%27_Medici_and_Luigi_de%27_Rossi_%28by_Raphael%29.jpg/440px-Portrait_of_Pope_Leo_X_and_his_cousins%2C_cardinals_Giulio_de%27_Medici_and_Luigi_de%27_Rossi_%28by_Raphael%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2538" data-file-height="3360" /></a><figcaption>Giulio Cardinal de' Medici, left; with his cousin <a href="/wiki/Pope_Leo_X" title="Pope Leo X">Pope Leo X</a>, center; and Luigi Cardinal de' Rossi, right; by <a href="/wiki/Raphael" title="Raphael">Raphael</a>, 1519.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Giulio de' Medici appeared on the world stage in March 1513, at the age of 35,<sup id="cite_ref-catholic-hierarchy.org_2-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-catholic-hierarchy.org-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> when his cousin Giovanni de' Medici was elected Pope, taking the name Leo X. Pope Leo X reigned until his death on 1 December 1521.
</p><p>"Learned, clever, respectable, and industrious," Giulio de’ Medici's reputation and responsibilities grew at a rapid pace, unusual even for the Renaissance.<sup id="cite_ref-newadvent.org_8-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-newadvent.org-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> Within three months of Leo X's election, he was named <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Archdiocese_of_Florence" title="Roman Catholic Archdiocese of Florence">Archbishop of Florence</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> Later that autumn, all barriers to his attaining the Church's highest offices were removed by a papal dispensation declaring his birth legitimate. It stated that his parents had been betrothed <span title="Latin-language text"><i lang="la">per sponsalia de presenti</i></span>, (i.e. "wed according to the word of those present.")<sup id="cite_ref-newadvent.org_8-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-newadvent.org-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> Whether or not this was true, it allowed Leo X to create him <a href="/wiki/Cardinal_(Catholicism)" class="mw-redirect" title="Cardinal (Catholicism)">cardinal</a> during the first papal consistory on 23 September 1513.<sup id="cite_ref-newadvent.org_8-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-newadvent.org-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> On 29 September, he was appointed Cardinal Deacon of Santa Maria in Dominica—a position that had been vacated by the Pope.<sup id="cite_ref-catholic-hierarchy.org_2-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-catholic-hierarchy.org-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Cardinal Giulio's reputation during the reign of Leo X is recorded by contemporary Marco Minio, the Venetian ambassador to the Papal Court, who wrote in a letter to the Venetian Senate in 1519: "Cardinal de' Medici, the Pope's <a href="/wiki/Cardinal_nephew" class="mw-redirect" title="Cardinal nephew">cardinal nephew</a>, who is not legitimate, has great power with the Pope; he is a man of great competence and great authority; he resides with the Pope, and does nothing of importance without first consulting him. But he is returning to Florence to govern the city."<sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Statesmanship">Statesmanship</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Clement_VII&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Statesmanship">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>While Cardinal Giulio was not officially appointed Vice-Chancellor of the Church (second-in-command) until 9 March 1517, in practice Leo X governed in partnership with his cousin from the beginning.<sup id="cite_ref-newadvent.org_8-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-newadvent.org-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> Initially, his duties centered primarily on administering Church affairs in Florence and conducting international relations. In January 1514, <a href="/wiki/King_Henry_VIII_of_England" class="mw-redirect" title="King Henry VIII of England">King Henry VIII of England</a> appointed him <a href="/wiki/Cardinal_protector_of_England" title="Cardinal protector of England">Cardinal protector of England</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-reformation500.csl.edu_29-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-reformation500.csl.edu-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> The following year, <a href="/wiki/King_Francis_I_of_France" class="mw-redirect" title="King Francis I of France">King Francis I of France</a> nominated him to become <a href="/wiki/Archbishop_of_Narbonne" class="mw-redirect" title="Archbishop of Narbonne">Archbishop of Narbonne</a>, and in 1516 named him cardinal protector of France.<sup id="cite_ref-reformation500.csl.edu_29-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-reformation500.csl.edu-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> In a scenario typical of Cardinal Giulio's independent-minded statesmanship, the respective kings of England and France, recognizing a conflict of interest in Giulio protecting both countries simultaneously, brought pressure to bear on him to resign his other protectorship; to their dismay, he refused.<sup id="cite_ref-auto3_30-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto3-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Cardinal Giulio's foreign policy was shaped by the idea of "la libertà d'Italia," which aimed to free Italy and the Church from French and Imperial domination.<sup id="cite_ref-auto7_23-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto7-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> This became clear in 1521, when a personal rivalry between King Francis I and <a href="/wiki/Holy_Roman_Emperor_Charles_V" class="mw-redirect" title="Holy Roman Emperor Charles V">Holy Roman Emperor Charles V</a> boiled over into war in northern Italy.<sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> Francis I expected Giulio, France's cardinal protector, to support him; but Giulio perceived Francis as threatening the Church's independence—particularly the latter's control of Lombardy, and his use of the <a href="/wiki/Concordat_of_Bologna" title="Concordat of Bologna">Concordat of Bologna</a> to control the Church in France. At the time, the Church wanted Emperor Charles V to combat Lutheranism, then growing in Germany. So Cardinal Giulio negotiated an alliance on behalf of the Church, to support the Holy Roman Empire against France.<sup id="cite_ref-csun.edu_32-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-csun.edu-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> That autumn, Giulio helped lead a victorious Imperial-Papal army over the French in Milan and Lombardy.<sup id="cite_ref-csun.edu_32-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-csun.edu-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> While his strategy of shifting alliances to liberate the Church and Italy from foreign domination proved disastrous during his reign as Pope Clement VII, during the reign of Leo X it skillfully maintained a balance of power among the competing international factions seeking to influence the Church.<sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Armed_conflicts">Armed conflicts</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Clement_VII&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Armed conflicts">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>Giulio de’ Medici led numerous armed conflicts as a cardinal. Commenting on this, his contemporary <a href="/wiki/Francesco_Guicciardini" title="Francesco Guicciardini">Francesco Guicciardini</a> wrote that Cardinal Giulio was better suited to arms than to the priesthood.<sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> He served as papal legate to the army in a campaign against Francis I in 1515, alongside inventor <a href="/wiki/Leonardo_da_Vinci" title="Leonardo da Vinci">Leonardo da Vinci</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Strathern_35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Strathern-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Achievements">Achievements</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Clement_VII&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Achievements">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Transfiguration_Raphael.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/51/Transfiguration_Raphael.jpg/220px-Transfiguration_Raphael.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="332" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/51/Transfiguration_Raphael.jpg/330px-Transfiguration_Raphael.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/51/Transfiguration_Raphael.jpg/440px-Transfiguration_Raphael.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1067" data-file-height="1608" /></a><figcaption>The Transfiguration, by <a href="/wiki/Raphael" title="Raphael">Raphael</a>, 1520. Commissioned by Cardinal Giulio de’ Medici</figcaption></figure>
<p>Cardinal Giulio's other endeavors on behalf of Pope Leo X were similarly successful, such that "he had the credit of being the prime mover of papal policy throughout the whole of Leo's pontificate."<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceA_36-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceA-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> In 1513, he was member of the <a href="/wiki/Fifth_Lateran_Council" class="mw-redirect" title="Fifth Lateran Council">Fifth Lateran Council</a>, assigned the task of healing the schism caused by <a href="/wiki/Conciliarism" title="Conciliarism">conciliarism</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-auto7_23-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto7-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> In 1515, his "most significant act of ecclesiastical government" regulated prophetic preaching in the manner of <a href="/wiki/Girolamo_Savonarola" title="Girolamo Savonarola">Girolamo Savonarola</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-auto7_23-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto7-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> He later organized and presided over the Florentine Synod of 1517, where he became the first member of the Church to implement the reforms recommended by the Fifth Lateran Council.<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceA_36-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceA-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> These included prohibiting priests from carrying arms, frequenting taverns, and dancing provocatively – while urging them to attend weekly confession.<sup id="cite_ref-Ashgate_7-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ashgate-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> Similarly, Cardinal Giulio's artistic patronage was admired (e.g., his commissioning <a href="/wiki/Raphael" title="Raphael">Raphael</a>’s <i><a href="/wiki/Transfiguration_(Raphael)" title="Transfiguration (Raphael)">Transfiguration</a></i> and <a href="/wiki/Michelangelo" title="Michelangelo">Michelangelo</a>’s <i><a href="/wiki/Medici_Chapel" title="Medici Chapel">Medici Chapel</a></i>, among other works), particularly for what goldsmith <a href="/wiki/Benvenuto_Cellini" title="Benvenuto Cellini">Benvenuto Cellini</a> later called its "excellent taste".<sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Gran_Maestro_of_Florence">Gran Maestro of Florence</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Clement_VII&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Gran Maestro of Florence">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Cardinal Giulio governed Florence between 1519 and 1523, following the death of its civic ruler, <a href="/wiki/Lorenzo_II_de_Medici" class="mw-redirect" title="Lorenzo II de Medici">Lorenzo II de Medici</a>, in 1519. There "he was permitted to assume almost autocratic control of State affairs," and "did very much to place public interests upon a firm and practical basis."<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> U.S. <a href="/wiki/President_John_Adams" class="mw-redirect" title="President John Adams">President John Adams</a> later characterized Giulio's administration of Florence as "very successful and frugal."<sup id="cite_ref-oll.libertyfund.org_5-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-oll.libertyfund.org-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> Adams chronicles the cardinal as having "reduced the business of the magistrates, elections, customs of office, and the mode of expenditure of public money, in such a manner that it produced a great and universal joy among the citizens."<sup id="cite_ref-oll.libertyfund.org_5-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-oll.libertyfund.org-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Ashgate_7-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ashgate-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On the death of Pope Leo X in 1521, Adams writes there was a "ready inclination in all of the principal citizens [of Florence], and a universal desire among the people, to maintain the state in the hands of the Cardinal de’ Medici; and all this felicity arose from his good government, which since the death of the Duke Lorenzo, had been universally agreeable."<sup id="cite_ref-oll.libertyfund.org_5-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-oll.libertyfund.org-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Under_Pope_Adrian_VI">Under Pope Adrian VI</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Clement_VII&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Under Pope Adrian VI">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Paus_Adrianus_VI_(1459-1523)_Rijksmuseum_SK-A-513.jpeg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0b/Paus_Adrianus_VI_%281459-1523%29_Rijksmuseum_SK-A-513.jpeg/220px-Paus_Adrianus_VI_%281459-1523%29_Rijksmuseum_SK-A-513.jpeg" decoding="async" width="220" height="267" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0b/Paus_Adrianus_VI_%281459-1523%29_Rijksmuseum_SK-A-513.jpeg/330px-Paus_Adrianus_VI_%281459-1523%29_Rijksmuseum_SK-A-513.jpeg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/0/0b/Paus_Adrianus_VI_%281459-1523%29_Rijksmuseum_SK-A-513.jpeg 2x" data-file-width="395" data-file-height="480" /></a><figcaption>Portrait of Pope Adrian VI in the <a href="/wiki/Rijksmuseum" title="Rijksmuseum">Rijksmuseum</a>.</figcaption></figure>
<p>When Pope Leo X died on 1 December 1521, Cardinal Giulio was "widely expected to succeed him"—but instead, during the conclave of 1522, the College of Cardinals elected a compromise candidate, <a href="/wiki/Adrian_VI" class="mw-redirect" title="Adrian VI">Adrian VI</a> of the Netherlands.<sup id="cite_ref-Strathern_35-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Strathern-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> Of why this happened, historian Paul Strathern writes, "it was common knowledge that [Cardinal Giulio] had been Leo X's most able adviser, as well as manager of the pope's financial affairs. The fact that Leo X had blithely ignored his cousin's advice, on so many occasions, was widely seen as being responsible for the plight of the papacy—not the influence of Cardinal Giulio de’ Medici. On the contrary, Cardinal Giulio appeared to be everything that Leo X was not: he was handsome, thoughtful, saturnine and gifted with good taste. Despite this, many remained steadfast in their opposition to his candidacy."<sup id="cite_ref-Strathern_35-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Strathern-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In conclave, Cardinal Giulio controlled the largest voting bloc, but his enemies forced the election to a stalemate.<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceB_39-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceB-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> Among them were Cardinal Francesco Soderini, a Florentine whose family had lost a power struggle to the Medici "and held a grudge"; Cardinal Pompeo Colonna, a Roman nobleman who wanted to become Pope himself; and a group of French cardinals who "were unwilling to forget Leo X's treachery to their King."<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceB_39-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceB-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Strathern_35-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Strathern-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Realizing that his candidacy was in jeopardy, "Cardinal Giulio now chose to make an astute tactical move. He declared modestly that he was unworthy of such high office; instead, he suggested the little-known Flemish scholar Cardinal Adrian Dedel, an ascetic and deeply spiritual man who had been tutor to the Holy Roman Emperor Charles V. Cardinal Giulio was sure that Cardinal Dedel would be rejected—on the grounds of his obscurity, his lack of political expertise and the fact that he was not Italian. The selfless suggestion that had been made by Cardinal Giulio de’ Medici would then demonstrate to all that he was in fact the ideal candidate. But this move backfired badly, Cardinal Giulio's bluff was called and Cardinal Adrian Dedel was elected as Pope Adrian VI."<sup id="cite_ref-Strathern_35-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Strathern-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During his 20-month papacy, Adrian VI "seemed to set great store by Cardinal Medici's opinions... And all the other cardinals were kept distinctly at arm's-length."<sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> In this way, Cardinal Giulio "wielded formidable influence" throughout Adrian's reign.<sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> Splitting time between the Palazzo Medici in Florence and the <a href="/wiki/Palazzo_della_Cancelleria" title="Palazzo della Cancelleria">Palazzo della Cancelleria</a> in Rome, Cardinal Giulio "lived there as a generous Medici was expected to live, a patron of artists and musicians, a protector of the poor, a lavish host."<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Assassination_Plot_of_1522">Assassination Plot of 1522</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Clement_VII&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Assassination Plot of 1522">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>In 1522, rumors began to swirl that Cardinal Giulio—lacking legitimate successors to rule Florence—planned to abdicate rule of the city and "leave the government freely in the people."<sup id="cite_ref-oll.libertyfund.org_5-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-oll.libertyfund.org-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> When it became clear that these rumors were untrue, a faction of mostly elite Florentines hatched a plot to assassinate him and then install their own government under his "great adversary", Cardinal <a href="/wiki/Francesco_Soderini" title="Francesco Soderini">Francesco Soderini</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-webdept.fiu.edu_43-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-webdept.fiu.edu-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-oll.libertyfund.org_5-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-oll.libertyfund.org-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> Soderini encouraged the plot, exhorting both Adrian and <a href="/wiki/Francis_I_of_France" title="Francis I of France">Francis I of France</a> to strike against Giulio and invade his allies in Sicily. This did not happen. Instead of breaking with Giulio, Adrian had Cardinal Soderini imprisoned.<sup id="cite_ref-webdept.fiu.edu_43-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-webdept.fiu.edu-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> Afterward, the principal conspirators were "declared rebels", and some were "apprehended and beheaded; by which means Giulio was again secured [as leader of Florence]."<sup id="cite_ref-oll.libertyfund.org_5-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-oll.libertyfund.org-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Pope">Pope</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Clement_VII&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Pope">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Following Adrian VI's death on 14 September 1523, Cardinal Giulio overcame the opposition of the French king<sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> and finally succeeded in being elected Pope Clement VII in the next <a href="/wiki/Papal_conclave,_1523" class="mw-redirect" title="Papal conclave, 1523">conclave</a> (19 November 1523).<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Pope Clement VII brought to the papal throne a high reputation for political ability and possessed in fact all the accomplishments of a wily diplomat. But his contemporaries considered him worldly and indifferent to the perceived dangers of the <a href="/wiki/Protestant_Reformation" class="mw-redirect" title="Protestant Reformation">Protestant Reformation</a>.
</p><p>At his accession, Clement VII sent the <a href="/wiki/Archbishop_of_Capua" class="mw-redirect" title="Archbishop of Capua">Archbishop of Capua</a>, <a href="/wiki/Nikolaus_von_Sch%C3%B6nberg" title="Nikolaus von Schönberg">Nikolaus von Schönberg</a>, to the Kings of <a href="/wiki/Early_modern_France" class="mw-redirect" title="Early modern France">France</a>, <a href="/wiki/Spain" title="Spain">Spain</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_England" title="Kingdom of England">England</a>, in order to bring the <a href="/wiki/Italian_War_of_1521%E2%80%931526" title="Italian War of 1521–1526">Italian War</a> to an end. An early report from the Protonotary Marino Ascanio Caracciolo<sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> to the Emperor records: "As the Turks threaten to conquer Christian states, it seems to him that it is his first duty as Pope to bring about a general peace of all Christian princes, and he begs him (the Emperor), as the firstborn son of the Church, to aid him in this pious work."<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> But the pope's attempt failed.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Continental_and_Medici_politics">Continental and Medici politics</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Clement_VII&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Continental and Medici politics">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Giuliano_Bugiardini_-_Ritratto_di_papa_Clemente_VII.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/94/Giuliano_Bugiardini_-_Ritratto_di_papa_Clemente_VII.jpg/220px-Giuliano_Bugiardini_-_Ritratto_di_papa_Clemente_VII.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="271" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/94/Giuliano_Bugiardini_-_Ritratto_di_papa_Clemente_VII.jpg/330px-Giuliano_Bugiardini_-_Ritratto_di_papa_Clemente_VII.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/94/Giuliano_Bugiardini_-_Ritratto_di_papa_Clemente_VII.jpg/440px-Giuliano_Bugiardini_-_Ritratto_di_papa_Clemente_VII.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1313" data-file-height="1615" /></a><figcaption>Portrait of Clement VII by <a href="/wiki/Giuliano_Bugiardini" title="Giuliano Bugiardini">Giuliano Bugiardini</a> (c. 1532)</figcaption></figure>
<p><a href="/wiki/Francis_I_of_France" title="Francis I of France">Francis I of France</a>'s conquest of Milan in 1524, during his <a href="/wiki/Italian_campaign_of_1524%E2%80%931525" title="Italian campaign of 1524–1525">Italian campaign of 1524–1525</a>, prompted the Pope to quit the <a href="/wiki/Holy_Roman_Empire" title="Holy Roman Empire">Imperial</a>–Spanish side and to ally himself with other Italian princes, including the <a href="/wiki/Republic_of_Venice" title="Republic of Venice">Republic of Venice</a>, and France in January 1525. This treaty granted the definitive acquisition of <a href="/wiki/Parma" title="Parma">Parma</a> and <a href="/wiki/Piacenza" title="Piacenza">Piacenza</a> for the <a href="/wiki/Papal_States" title="Papal States">Papal States</a>, the rule of Medici over Florence and the free passage of the French troops to <a href="/wiki/Naples" title="Naples">Naples</a>. This policy in itself was sound and patriotic, but Clement VII's zeal soon cooled; by his want of foresight and unseasonable economy, he laid himself open to an attack from the turbulent Roman barons, which obliged him to invoke the mediation of the emperor, Charles V. One month later, Francis I was crushed and imprisoned in the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Pavia" title="Battle of Pavia">Battle of Pavia</a>, and Clement VII went deeper in his former engagements with Charles V, signing an alliance with the <a href="/wiki/Charles_de_Lannoy" class="mw-redirect" title="Charles de Lannoy">viceroy of Naples</a>.
</p><p>But deeply concerned about Imperial arrogance, he was to pick up with France again when Francis I was freed after the <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Madrid_(1526)" class="mw-redirect" title="Treaty of Madrid (1526)">Treaty of Madrid (1526)</a>: the Pope entered into the <a href="/wiki/League_of_Cognac" class="mw-redirect" title="League of Cognac">League of Cognac</a> together with France, <a href="/wiki/Republic_of_Venice" title="Republic of Venice">Venice</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Francesco_II_Sforza" title="Francesco II Sforza">Francesco II Sforza</a> of <a href="/wiki/Duchy_of_Milan" title="Duchy of Milan">Milan</a>. Clement VII issued an invective against Charles V, who in reply defined him a "wolf" instead of a "shepherd", menacing the summoning of a council about the <a href="/wiki/Lutheran" class="mw-redirect" title="Lutheran">Lutheran</a> question.
</p><p>Like his cousin Pope Leo X, Clement was considered too generous to his Medici relatives, draining the Vatican treasuries. This included the assignment of positions all the way up to Cardinal, lands, titles, and money. These actions prompted reform measures after Clement's death to help prevent such excessive nepotism.<sup id="cite_ref-Tomas_48-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Tomas-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Evangelization">Evangelization</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Clement_VII&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Evangelization">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In his 1529 bull "<a href="/wiki/Intra_Arcana" title="Intra Arcana">Intra Arcana</a>" Clement VII gave a grant of permissions and privileges to Charles V and the Spanish Empire, which included the power of <a href="/wiki/Jus_patronatus" title="Jus patronatus">patronage</a> within their colonies in the Americas.<sup id="cite_ref-Stogre_49-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Stogre-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Sack_of_Rome">Sack of Rome</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Clement_VII&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Sack of Rome">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Sack_of_Rome_(1527)" title="Sack of Rome (1527)">Sack of Rome (1527)</a></div>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1097763485">.mw-parser-output .ambox{border:1px solid #a2a9b1;border-left:10px solid #36c;background-color:#fbfbfb;box-sizing:border-box}.mw-parser-output .mbox-text-span{margin-left:23px!important}@media(min-width:720px){.mw-parser-output .ambox{margin:0 10%}}</style><table class="box-More_citations_needed_section plainlinks metadata ambox ambox-content ambox-Refimprove" role="presentation"><tbody><tr><td class="mbox-image"><div class="mbox-image-div"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:Question_book-new.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/9/99/Question_book-new.svg/50px-Question_book-new.svg.png" decoding="async" width="50" height="39"
