//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/9/99/Question_book-new.svg/100px-Question_book-new.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="512" data-file-height="399" /></a></span></div></td><td class="mbox-text"><div class="mbox-text-span">This section <b>needs additional citations for <a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability" title="Wikipedia:Verifiability">verification</a></b>.<span class="hide-when-compact"> Please help <a href="/wiki/Special:EditPage/Pope_Clement_VII" title="Special:EditPage/Pope Clement VII">improve this article</a> by <a href="/wiki/Help:Referencing_for_beginners" title="Help:Referencing for beginners">adding citations to reliable sources</a>&#32;in this section. Unsourced material may be challenged and removed.</span>  <span class="date-container"><i>(<span class="date">March 2016</span>)</i></span><span class="hide-when-compact"><i> (<small><a href="/wiki/Help:Maintenance_template_removal" title="Help:Maintenance template removal">Learn how and when to remove this template message</a></small>)</i></span></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p>The Pope's wavering politics also caused the rise of the Imperial party inside the <a href="/wiki/Roman_Curia" title="Roman Curia">Curia</a>: Cardinal <a href="/wiki/Pompeo_Colonna" title="Pompeo Colonna">Pompeo Colonna</a>'s soldiers pillaged <a href="/wiki/Vatican_Hill" title="Vatican Hill">Vatican Hill</a> and gained control of the whole of Rome in his name. The humiliated Pope promised therefore to bring the Papal States to the Imperial side again. But soon after, Colonna left the siege and went to Naples, not keeping his promises and dismissing the Cardinal from his charge.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Category:Articles_contradicting_other_articles" title="Category:Articles contradicting other articles"><span title="This text contradicts material elsewhere on this page. (March 2016)">contradictory</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> From this point on, Clement VII could do nothing but follow the fate of the French party to the end.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Please_clarify" title="Wikipedia:Please clarify"><span title="The text near this tag is ambiguous, and needs clarification. (March 2016)">ambiguous</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>Soon he found himself alone in Italy too, as <a href="/wiki/Alfonso_d%27Este" class="mw-redirect" title="Alfonso d&#39;Este">Alfonso d'Este</a>, duke of Ferrara, had supplied artillery to the Imperial army, causing the League Army to keep a distance behind the horde of <a href="/wiki/Landsknecht" title="Landsknecht">Landsknechts</a> led by <a href="/wiki/Charles_III,_Duke_of_Bourbon" title="Charles III, Duke of Bourbon">Charles III, Duke of Bourbon</a> and <a href="/wiki/Georg_von_Frundsberg" title="Georg von Frundsberg">Georg von Frundsberg</a>, allowing them to reach Rome without harm.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Accuracy_dispute#Disputed_statement" title="Wikipedia:Accuracy dispute"><span title="The material near this tag is possibly inaccurate or nonfactual. (March 2016)">dubious</span></a>&#32;<span class="metadata"> &#8211; <a href="/wiki/Talk:Pope_Clement_VII#Dubious" title="Talk:Pope Clement VII">discuss</a></span></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Castel_Sant%27Angelo_bild.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8d/Castel_Sant%27Angelo_bild.jpg/220px-Castel_Sant%27Angelo_bild.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="173" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8d/Castel_Sant%27Angelo_bild.jpg/330px-Castel_Sant%27Angelo_bild.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8d/Castel_Sant%27Angelo_bild.jpg/440px-Castel_Sant%27Angelo_bild.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1955" data-file-height="1537" /></a><figcaption>Castel Sant'Angelo</figcaption></figure>
<p>Charles of Bourbon died while mounting a ladder during the short siege and his starving troops, unpaid and left without a guide, felt free to ravage Rome from 6 May 1527. The many incidents of murder, rape, and vandalism that followed ended the splendours of <a href="/wiki/Italian_Renaissance" title="Italian Renaissance">Renaissance Rome</a> forever. Clement VII, who had displayed no more resolution in his military than in his political conduct, was shortly afterwards (6 June) obliged to surrender himself together with the <a href="/wiki/Castel_Sant%27Angelo" title="Castel Sant&#39;Angelo">Castel Sant'Angelo</a>, where he had taken refuge. He agreed to pay a ransom of 400,000 <a href="/wiki/Ducato_(coin)" class="mw-redirect" title="Ducato (coin)">ducati</a> in exchange for his life; conditions included the cession of <a href="/wiki/Parma" title="Parma">Parma</a>, <a href="/wiki/Piacenza" title="Piacenza">Piacenza</a>, <a href="/wiki/Civitavecchia" title="Civitavecchia">Civitavecchia</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Modena" title="Modena">Modena</a> to the Holy Roman Empire. (Only the last could be occupied in fact.) At the same time, Venice took advantage of his situation to capture <a href="/wiki/Cervia" title="Cervia">Cervia</a> and <a href="/wiki/Ravenna" title="Ravenna">Ravenna</a> while <a href="/wiki/Sigismondo_Malatesta" title="Sigismondo Malatesta">Sigismondo Malatesta</a> returned in <a href="/wiki/Rimini" title="Rimini">Rimini</a>.
</p><p>Clement was kept as a prisoner in Castel Sant'Angelo for six months. After having bought off some Imperial officers, he escaped disguised as a peddler and took shelter in <a href="/wiki/Orvieto" title="Orvieto">Orvieto</a> and then in <a href="/wiki/Viterbo" title="Viterbo">Viterbo</a>. He came back to a depopulated and devastated Rome only in October 1528.
</p><p>Meanwhile, in Florence, Republican enemies of the Medici took advantage of the chaos to again expel the Pope's family from the city.
</p><p>In June 1529 the warring parties signed the <a href="/wiki/Peace_of_Barcelona" class="mw-redirect" title="Peace of Barcelona">Peace of Barcelona</a>. The Papal States regained some cities and Charles V agreed to restore the Medici to power in Florence. In 1530, after an eleven-month <a href="/wiki/Siege_of_Florence_(1529%E2%80%931530)" title="Siege of Florence (1529–1530)">siege</a>, the Tuscan city capitulated and Clement VII installed his illegitimate nephew <a href="/wiki/Alessandro_de_Medici" class="mw-redirect" title="Alessandro de Medici">Alessandro</a> as duke. Subsequently, the Pope followed a policy of subservience to the emperor, endeavouring on the one hand to induce him to act with severity against the Lutherans in Germany and on the other to avoid his demands for a general council.
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Appearance">Appearance</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Clement_VII&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Appearance">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Sebastiano_del_Piombo_%E2%80%93_Portrait_of_Pope_Clement_VII_(ca._1526).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ea/Sebastiano_del_Piombo_%E2%80%93_Portrait_of_Pope_Clement_VII_%28ca._1526%29.jpg/220px-Sebastiano_del_Piombo_%E2%80%93_Portrait_of_Pope_Clement_VII_%28ca._1526%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="326" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ea/Sebastiano_del_Piombo_%E2%80%93_Portrait_of_Pope_Clement_VII_%28ca._1526%29.jpg/330px-Sebastiano_del_Piombo_%E2%80%93_Portrait_of_Pope_Clement_VII_%28ca._1526%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ea/Sebastiano_del_Piombo_%E2%80%93_Portrait_of_Pope_Clement_VII_%28ca._1526%29.jpg/440px-Sebastiano_del_Piombo_%E2%80%93_Portrait_of_Pope_Clement_VII_%28ca._1526%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2441" data-file-height="3615" /></a><figcaption>Clement VII, age 48<br />Portrait by <a href="/wiki/Sebastiano_del_Piombo" title="Sebastiano del Piombo">Sebastiano del Piombo</a>, 1526</figcaption></figure>
<p>During his half-year imprisonment in 1527, Clement VII grew a full beard as a sign of mourning for the <a href="/wiki/Sack_of_Rome_(1527)" title="Sack of Rome (1527)">sack of Rome</a>. This was in contradiction to Catholic canon law,<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> which required priests to be clean-shaven but had as precedent the beard <a href="/wiki/Pope_Julius_II" title="Pope Julius II">Pope Julius II</a> wore for nine months in 1511–12 as a sign of mourning for the papal city of <a href="/wiki/Bologna" title="Bologna">Bologna</a>.
</p><p>Unlike Julius II, however, Clement kept his beard until his death in 1534. His example in wearing a beard was followed by his successor, <a href="/wiki/Paul_III" class="mw-redirect" title="Paul III">Paul III</a>, and indeed by 24 popes after him, down to <a href="/wiki/Innocent_XII" class="mw-redirect" title="Innocent XII">Innocent XII</a>, who died in 1700. Clement was thus the unintentional originator of a fashion that lasted well over a century.
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Ancona">Ancona</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Clement_VII&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Ancona">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>In 1532, Clement VII took possession of <a href="/wiki/Ancona" title="Ancona">Ancona</a>, which definitively lost its freedom and became part of the <a href="/wiki/Papal_States" title="Papal States">Papal States</a>, ending hundreds of years when the <a href="/wiki/Republic_of_Ancona" title="Republic of Ancona">Republic of Ancona</a> was an important maritime power.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="English_Reformation">English Reformation</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Clement_VII&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: English Reformation">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Charles_V_enthroned_over_his_defeated_enemies_Giulio_Clovio_mid_16th_century.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e0/Charles_V_enthroned_over_his_defeated_enemies_Giulio_Clovio_mid_16th_century.jpg/330px-Charles_V_enthroned_over_his_defeated_enemies_Giulio_Clovio_mid_16th_century.jpg" decoding="async" width="330" height="216" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e0/Charles_V_enthroned_over_his_defeated_enemies_Giulio_Clovio_mid_16th_century.jpg/495px-Charles_V_enthroned_over_his_defeated_enemies_Giulio_Clovio_mid_16th_century.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e0/Charles_V_enthroned_over_his_defeated_enemies_Giulio_Clovio_mid_16th_century.jpg/660px-Charles_V_enthroned_over_his_defeated_enemies_Giulio_Clovio_mid_16th_century.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2600" data-file-height="1700" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Charles_V,_Holy_Roman_Emperor" title="Charles V, Holy Roman Emperor">Charles V</a>, enthroned over his defeated enemies (from left): <a href="/wiki/Suleiman_the_Magnificent" title="Suleiman the Magnificent">Suleiman the Magnificent</a>, Pope Clement VII, <a href="/wiki/Francis_I_of_France" title="Francis I of France">Francis I of France</a>, the <a href="/wiki/William,_Duke_of_J%C3%BClich-Cleves-Berg" title="William, Duke of Jülich-Cleves-Berg">Duke of Cleves</a>, the <a href="/wiki/John_Frederick_I,_Elector_of_Saxony" title="John Frederick I, Elector of Saxony">Duke of Saxony</a>, and the <a href="/wiki/Philip_I,_Landgrave_of_Hesse" title="Philip I, Landgrave of Hesse">Landgrave of Hesse</a>. <a href="/wiki/Giulio_Clovio" title="Giulio Clovio">Giulio Clovio</a>, mid-16th century</figcaption></figure>
<p>By the late 1520s, <a href="/wiki/Henry_VIII_of_England" class="mw-redirect" title="Henry VIII of England">King Henry VIII</a> wanted to have his marriage to Charles's aunt <a href="/wiki/Catherine_of_Aragon" title="Catherine of Aragon">Catherine of Aragon</a> <a href="/wiki/Declaration_of_nullity" title="Declaration of nullity">annulled</a>. The couple's sons died in infancy, threatening the future of the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Tudor" title="House of Tudor">House of Tudor</a>, although Henry did have a daughter, <a href="/wiki/Mary_I_of_England" title="Mary I of England">Mary Tudor</a>. Henry claimed that this lack of a male heir was because his marriage was "blighted in the eyes of God".<sup id="cite_ref-Phillips_52-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Phillips-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> Catherine had been his <a href="/wiki/Arthur,_Prince_of_Wales" title="Arthur, Prince of Wales">brother</a>'s widow, but the marriage had been childless, so the marriage was not against Old Testament law, which forbids such unions only if the brother had children.<sup id="cite_ref-Leviticus_20:21_53-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Leviticus_20:21-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> Moreover, <a href="/wiki/Pope_Julius_II" title="Pope Julius II">Pope Julius II</a> had given a dispensation to allow the wedding.<sup id="cite_ref-Lacey_54-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Lacey-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> Henry now argued that this had been wrong and that his marriage had never been valid. In 1527 Henry asked Clement to annul the marriage, but the Pope, possibly acting under pressure from Catherine's nephew, Holy Roman Emperor Charles V, whose effective prisoner he was, refused. According to Catholic teaching, a validly contracted marriage is indivisible until death, and thus the pope cannot annul a marriage on the basis of an <a href="/wiki/Impediment_(Catholic_canon_law)" title="Impediment (Catholic canon law)">impediment</a> previously <a href="/wiki/Dispensation_(canon_law)" class="mw-redirect" title="Dispensation (canon law)">dispensed</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Scarisbrick2011_55-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Scarisbrick2011-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> Many people close to Henry wished simply to ignore Clement, but in October 1530 a meeting of clergy and lawyers advised that the English Parliament could not empower the <a href="/wiki/Archbishop_of_Canterbury" title="Archbishop of Canterbury">Archbishop of Canterbury</a> to act against the Pope's prohibition. In Parliament, Bishop <a href="/wiki/John_Fisher" title="John Fisher">John Fisher</a> was the Pope's champion.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Lead-alloy_papal_bulla_issued_under_Clement_VII_(FindID_869584).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6d/Lead-alloy_papal_bulla_issued_under_Clement_VII_%28FindID_869584%29.jpg/220px-Lead-alloy_papal_bulla_issued_under_Clement_VII_%28FindID_869584%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="121" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6d/Lead-alloy_papal_bulla_issued_under_Clement_VII_%28FindID_869584%29.jpg/330px-Lead-alloy_papal_bulla_issued_under_Clement_VII_%28FindID_869584%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6d/Lead-alloy_papal_bulla_issued_under_Clement_VII_%28FindID_869584%29.jpg/440px-Lead-alloy_papal_bulla_issued_under_Clement_VII_%28FindID_869584%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3030" data-file-height="1660" /></a><figcaption>Lead <a href="/wiki/Bulla_(seal)" title="Bulla (seal)">bulla</a> of Clement VII, found in <a href="/wiki/Hertfordshire" title="Hertfordshire">Hertfordshire</a>, England</figcaption></figure>
<p>Henry subsequently underwent a marriage ceremony with <a href="/wiki/Anne_Boleyn" title="Anne Boleyn">Anne Boleyn</a>, in either late 1532 or early 1533.<sup id="cite_ref-Ives_56-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ives-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> The marriage was made easier by the death of the Archbishop of Canterbury <a href="/wiki/William_Warham" title="William Warham">William Warham</a>, a stalwart friend of the Pope, after which Henry persuaded Clement to appoint <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Cranmer" title="Thomas Cranmer">Thomas Cranmer</a>, a friend of the Boleyn family, as his successor. The Pope granted the <a href="/wiki/Papal_bull" title="Papal bull">papal bulls</a> necessary for Cranmer's promotion to Canterbury, and also demanded that Cranmer take the customary oath of allegiance to the pope before his consecration. Laws made under Henry already declared that bishops would be consecrated even without papal approval. Cranmer was consecrated, while declaring beforehand that he did not agree with the oath he would take.<sup id="cite_ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> Cranmer was prepared to grant the annulment<sup id="cite_ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> of the marriage to Catherine as Henry required. The Pope responded to the marriage by <a href="/wiki/Excommunication_(Catholic_Church)" class="mw-redirect" title="Excommunication (Catholic Church)">excommunicating</a> both Henry and Cranmer from the Catholic Church.
</p><p>Consequently, in England, in the same year, the <a href="/wiki/Act_Concerning_Ecclesiastical_Appointments_and_Absolute_Restraint_of_Annates" class="mw-redirect" title="Act Concerning Ecclesiastical Appointments and Absolute Restraint of Annates">Act of Conditional Restraint of Annates</a> transferred the taxes on ecclesiastical income from the Pope to <a href="/wiki/The_Crown" title="The Crown">the Crown</a>. The <a href="/wiki/Act_Concerning_Peter%27s_Pence_and_Dispensations" class="mw-redirect" title="Act Concerning Peter&#39;s Pence and Dispensations">Peter's Pence Act</a> outlawed the annual payment by landowners of <a href="/wiki/Peter%27s_Pence" title="Peter&#39;s Pence">one penny</a> to the Pope. This act also reiterated that England had "no superior under God, but only your <a href="/wiki/Grace_(style)" title="Grace (style)">Grace</a>" and that Henry's "imperial crown" had been diminished by the Pope's "unreasonable and uncharitable usurpations and exactions".<sup id="cite_ref-Lehmberg_59-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Lehmberg-59">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> Ultimately, in 1534, Henry led the English Parliament to pass the <a href="/wiki/Acts_of_Supremacy#First_Act_of_Supremacy_1534" title="Acts of Supremacy">Act of Supremacy</a> that established the independent <a href="/wiki/Church_of_England" title="Church of England">Church of England</a> and broke from the Catholic Church.
</p>
<h3><span id="Marriage_of_Catherine_de.27_Medici"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Marriage_of_Catherine_de'_Medici">Marriage of Catherine de' Medici</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Clement_VII&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: Marriage of Catherine de&#039; Medici">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Meeting_of_Francis_I_and_Pope_Clement_VII_in_Marseilles_13_October_1533.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c9/Meeting_of_Francis_I_and_Pope_Clement_VII_in_Marseilles_13_October_1533.jpg/220px-Meeting_of_Francis_I_and_Pope_Clement_VII_in_Marseilles_13_October_1533.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="103" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c9/Meeting_of_Francis_I_and_Pope_Clement_VII_in_Marseilles_13_October_1533.jpg/330px-Meeting_of_Francis_I_and_Pope_Clement_VII_in_Marseilles_13_October_1533.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c9/Meeting_of_Francis_I_and_Pope_Clement_VII_in_Marseilles_13_October_1533.jpg/440px-Meeting_of_Francis_I_and_Pope_Clement_VII_in_Marseilles_13_October_1533.jpg 2x" data-file-width="9559" data-file-height="4475" /></a><figcaption>Meeting of <a href="/wiki/Francis_I_of_France" title="Francis I of France">Francis I</a> and Pope Clement VII in <a href="/wiki/Marseilles" class="mw-redirect" title="Marseilles">Marseilles</a>, 13 October 1533</figcaption></figure>
<p>In 1533, Clement married his cousin's granddaughter, <a href="/wiki/Catherine_de_Medici" class="mw-redirect" title="Catherine de Medici">Catherine de Medici</a>, to the future <a href="/wiki/Henry_II_of_France" title="Henry II of France">King Henry II</a> of France, son of King Francis I. Due to an illness, before setting out to <a href="/wiki/Marseilles" class="mw-redirect" title="Marseilles">Marseilles</a> for the wedding, Clement issued a Bull on 3 September 1533 giving instructions on what to do if he died outside Rome.<sup id="cite_ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> The wedding ceremony took place at Église Saint-Ferréol les Augustins on 28 October 1533 and was conducted by Clement himself. It was "followed by nine days of lavish banquets, pageants, and festivities."<sup id="cite_ref-Strathern_35-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Strathern-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> On 7 November in Marseilles, Clement created four new cardinals, all of them French.<sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup> He also held separate, private meetings with Francis I and Charles V. Charles’ daughter, <a href="/wiki/Margaret_of_Parma" title="Margaret of Parma">Margaret of Austria</a> was set to marry Clement's relative, Duke Alessandro de’ Medici in 1536.<sup id="cite_ref-Fletcher_62-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Fletcher-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to Medici historian Paul Strathern, Clement marrying Catherine into France's royal family; and Alessandro becoming Duke of Florence and marrying into the Hapsburg family, "marked perhaps the most significant turning point in the history of the Medici family—the ascent into nobility in Florence, and the joining of the French royal family. Without the guiding hand of Clement VII, the Medici would never have been able to achieve the pinnacles of greatness that were yet to come" in the following centuries.<sup id="cite_ref-Strathern_35-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Strathern-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Death">Death</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Clement_VII&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: Death">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>On 10 December 1533, Clement returned to Rome with a fever and complaining of stomach problems. Strathern writes of how he had been ill for months: "[he] was aging rapidly...his liver was failing and his skin turned yellow; he also lost the sight of one eye and became partially blind in the other."<sup id="cite_ref-Strathern_35-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Strathern-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> He was so ill at the beginning of August 1534 that Cardinal <a href="/wiki/Agostino_Trivulzio" title="Agostino Trivulzio">Agostino Trivulzio</a> wrote to King Francis that the Pope's doctors feared for his life.<sup id="cite_ref-63" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-63">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Later the following year, on 23 September 1534, Clement wrote a long letter of farewell to Emperor Charles.<sup id="cite_ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> He also affirmed, just days before his death, that <a href="/wiki/Michelangelo" title="Michelangelo">Michelangelo</a> should paint <i><a href="/wiki/The_Last_Judgment_(Michelangelo)" title="The Last Judgment (Michelangelo)">The Last Judgment</a></i> above the altar in the <a href="/wiki/Sistine_Chapel" title="Sistine Chapel">Sistine Chapel</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-catholic_18-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-catholic-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> Clement VII died just two days later, on 25 September 1534,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReynolds2016161_65-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReynolds2016161-65">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup> having lived 56 years and four months, reigning for 10 years, 10 months, and 7 days. His body was interred in Saint Peter's Basilica, and later transferred to a tomb in <a href="/wiki/Santa_Maria_sopra_Minerva" title="Santa Maria sopra Minerva">Santa Maria sopra Minerva</a> in Rome,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEVisceglia2006181_66-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEVisceglia2006181-66">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup> which was designed by <a href="/wiki/Baccio_Bandinelli" title="Baccio Bandinelli">Baccio Bandinelli</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHersey199356_67-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHersey199356-67">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Clement's biographer <a href="/wiki/Emmanuel_Rodocanachi" title="Emmanuel Rodocanachi">Emmanuel Rodocanachi</a> writes that "in accordance with the custom of those times, people attributed his death to poison"—specifically, poisoning by <a href="/wiki/Death_cap_mushroom" class="mw-redirect" title="Death cap mushroom">death cap mushroom</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-68" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-68">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-wasson_69-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-wasson-69">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Clement's symptoms and the length of his illness do not, however, support the hypothesis that he had been poisoned.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="margin-left:0.1em; white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Please_clarify" title="Wikipedia:Please clarify"><span title="The text near this tag may need clarification or removal of jargon. (August 2023)">clarification needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (August 2023)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Legacy">Legacy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Clement_VII&amp;action=edit&amp;section=20" title="Edit section: Legacy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Political_legacy">Political legacy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Clement_VII&amp;action=edit&amp;section=21" title="Edit section: Political legacy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Angelo_Bronzino_-_Portrait_of_Pope_Clement_VII_-_WGA3272.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/37/Angelo_Bronzino_-_Portrait_of_Pope_Clement_VII_-_WGA3272.jpg/220px-Angelo_Bronzino_-_Portrait_of_Pope_Clement_VII_-_WGA3272.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="280" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/37/Angelo_Bronzino_-_Portrait_of_Pope_Clement_VII_-_WGA3272.jpg/330px-Angelo_Bronzino_-_Portrait_of_Pope_Clement_VII_-_WGA3272.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/37/Angelo_Bronzino_-_Portrait_of_Pope_Clement_VII_-_WGA3272.jpg/440px-Angelo_Bronzino_-_Portrait_of_Pope_Clement_VII_-_WGA3272.jpg 2x" data-file-width="850" data-file-height="1083" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Agnolo_Bronzino" class="mw-redirect" title="Agnolo Bronzino">Agnolo Bronzino</a> – Portrait of Pope Clement VII</figcaption></figure>
<p>Clement VII's papacy is generally regarded as one of history's most tumultuous; opinions of Clement himself are often nuanced.<sup id="cite_ref-70" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-70">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup> For example, Clement's contemporary <a href="/wiki/Francesco_Vettori" title="Francesco Vettori">Francesco Vettori</a> writes that he "endured a great labor to become, from a great and respected cardinal, a small and little-esteemed pope", but also that "if one considers the lives of previous popes one may truly say that, for more than a hundred years, no better man than Clement VII sat upon the Throne. Nevertheless, it was in his day that the disaster took place while these others, who were filled with all vices, lived and died in felicity—as the world sees it. Neither should we seek to question the Lord, our God, who will punish—or not punish–in what manner and in what time it pleases him.’"<sup id="cite_ref-71" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-71">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The disasters of Clement's pontificate—the Sack of Rome and the English Reformation—are regarded as turning points in the histories of Catholicism, Europe, and the Renaissance.<sup id="cite_ref-72" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-72">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup> Modern historian Kenneth Gouwens writes, "Clement's failures must be viewed above all in the context of major changes in the dynamics of European politics. As warfare on the Italian peninsula intensified in the mid-1520s, the imperative of autonomy [for the Catholic Church and Italy] required enormous financial outlays to field standing armies. Political survival perforce eclipsed ecclesiastical reform as a short-term goal, and the costs of war necessitated the curtailment of expenditure on culture. Clement pursued policies consistent with those of his illustrious predecessors Julius II and Leo X; but in the 1520s, those policies could but fail.... Reform of the Church, to which his successors would turn, required resources and concerted secular support that the second Medici pope was unable to muster."<sup id="cite_ref-73" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-73">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Regarding Clement's struggle to liberate Italy and the Catholic Church from foreign domination, historian Fred Dotolo writes that "one might see in his papacy a vigorous defense of papal rights against the growth of monarchial power, a diplomatic and even pastoral struggle to retain the ancient division within Christendom of the priestly and kingly offices. Should the new monarchs of the early modern period reduce the papacy to a mere appendage of secular authority, religious issues would become little more than state policy.... Clement VII attempted to restrain the expansion of royal power and maintain the independence of Rome and of papal prerogatives."<sup id="cite_ref-74" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-74">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Ecclesiastically, Clement is remembered for orders protecting <a href="/wiki/Marrano" title="Marrano">Jews</a> from the <a href="/wiki/Portuguese_Inquisition" title="Portuguese Inquisition">Inquisition</a>, approving the <a href="/wiki/Theatine" class="mw-redirect" title="Theatine">Theatine</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-75" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-75">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Barnabite" class="mw-redirect" title="Barnabite">Barnabite</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-76" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-76">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/Capuchin_Franciscan_Order" class="mw-redirect" title="Capuchin Franciscan Order">Capuchin</a> Orders,<sup id="cite_ref-77" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-77">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup> and securing the island of <a href="/wiki/Malta" title="Malta">Malta</a> for the <a href="/wiki/Knights_of_Malta" class="mw-redirect" title="Knights of Malta">Knights of Malta</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-78" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-78">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-79" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-79">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-80" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-80">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-auto3_30-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto3-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In a final analysis of Clement's papacy, historian E.R. Chamberlin writes, "in all but his personal attributes, Clement VII was a protagonist in a Greek tragedy, the victim called upon to endure the results of actions committed long before. Each temporal claim of his predecessors had entangled the Papacy just a little more in the lethal game of politics, even while each moral debasement divorced it just a little more from the vast body of Christians from whom ultimately it drew its strength."<sup id="cite_ref-81" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-81">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup> More charitably, modern historian James Grubb writes, "indeed, at a certain point it is difficult to see how he might have fared much better, given the obstacles he faced. Certainly his predecessors since the end of the Schism had experienced their share of opposition, but did any have to fight on so many fronts as Clement, and against such overwhelming odds? At one time or another he battled the Holy Roman Empire (now fueled by precious metals from America), the French, the Turks, rival Italian powers, fractious forces within the papal states, and entrenched interests within the Curia itself. That the precious liberta d’Italia (freedom from outside domination) should have been lost irrevocably seems more an inevitability than a product of Clement's particular failings. He tried his utmost...."<sup id="cite_ref-82" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-82">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Portrayals">Portrayals</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Clement_VII&amp;action=edit&amp;section=22" title="Edit section: Portrayals">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>The life of the second Medici pope has been portrayed in numerous films and tv accounts, notably the Netflix show 'Medici; The Magnificent' where the figure is portrayed by British actor Jacob Dudman.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Patronage">Patronage</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Clement_VII&amp;action=edit&amp;section=23" title="Edit section: Patronage">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Last_Judgement_(Michelangelo).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/18/Last_Judgement_%28Michelangelo%29.jpg/220px-Last_Judgement_%28Michelangelo%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="242" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/18/Last_Judgement_%28Michelangelo%29.jpg/330px-Last_Judgement_%28Michelangelo%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/18/Last_Judgement_%28Michelangelo%29.jpg/440px-Last_Judgement_%28Michelangelo%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3991" data-file-height="4395" /></a><figcaption>The Last Judgement by <a href="/wiki/Michelangelo" title="Michelangelo">Michelangelo</a>, commissioned by Pope Clement VII.</figcaption></figure>
<p>As both a cardinal and Pope, Giulio de’ Medici "commissioned or supervised many of the best-known artistic undertakings of the <a href="/wiki/Cinquecento" title="Cinquecento">cinquecento</a>."<sup id="cite_ref-83" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-83">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup> Of those works, he's best known for Michelangelo's monumental fresco in the <a href="/wiki/Sistine_Chapel" title="Sistine Chapel">Sistine Chapel</a>, <i><a href="/wiki/The_Last_Judgment_(Michelangelo)" title="The Last Judgment (Michelangelo)">The Last Judgment</a></i>; <a href="/wiki/Raphael" title="Raphael">Raphael</a>’s iconic altarpiece <i><a href="/wiki/Transfiguration_(Raphael)" title="Transfiguration (Raphael)">The Transfiguration</a></i>; Michelangelo's sculptures for the <i><a href="/wiki/Medici_Chapel" title="Medici Chapel">Medici Chapel</a></i> in Florence; Raphael's architectural <i><a href="/wiki/Villa_Madama" title="Villa Madama">Villa Madama</a></i> in Rome; and Michelangelo's innovative <i><a href="/wiki/Laurentian_Library" title="Laurentian Library">Laurentian Library</a></i> in Florence.<sup id="cite_ref-84" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-84">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-85" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-85">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-86" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-86">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-87" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-87">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup> "As a patron, [Giulio de’ Medici] proved extraordinarily confident in technical affairs," which allowed him to suggest workable architectural and artistic solutions for commissions ranging from <a href="/wiki/Michelangelo" title="Michelangelo">Michelangelo</a>’s <i><a href="/wiki/Laurentian_Library" title="Laurentian Library">Laurentian Library</a></i> to <a href="/wiki/Benvenuto_Cellini" title="Benvenuto Cellini">Benvenuto Cellini</a>’s celebrated Papal Morse.<sup id="cite_ref-88" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-88">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-auto4_12-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto4-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-89" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-89">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup> As Pope, he appointed goldsmith Cellini head of the Papal Mint; and painter <a href="/wiki/Sebastiano_del_Piombo" title="Sebastiano del Piombo">Sebastiano del Piombo</a> keeper of the Papal Seal.<sup id="cite_ref-90" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-90">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-auto2_91-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto2-91">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup> Sebastiano's tour de force, <i><a href="/wiki/The_Raising_of_Lazarus_(Sebastiano_del_Piombo)" title="The Raising of Lazarus (Sebastiano del Piombo)">The Raising of Lazarus</a></i>, was produced via a contest arranged by Cardinal Giulio, pitting Sebastiano in direct competition with Raphael over who could produce the better altarpiece for the <a href="/wiki/Narbonne_Cathedral" title="Narbonne Cathedral">Narbonne Cathedral</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-auto2_91-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto2-91">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-92" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-92">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Giulio de’ Medici's patronage extended to theology, literature, and science. Some of the best known works associated with him are <a href="/wiki/Erasmus" title="Erasmus">Erasmus</a>’ <i><a href="/w/index.php?title=On_Free_Will&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="On Free Will (page does not exist)">On Free Will</a></i>, which he encouraged in response to <a href="/wiki/Martin_Luther" title="Martin Luther">Martin Luther</a>’s critiques of the Catholic Church; <a href="/wiki/Machiavelli" class="mw-redirect" title="Machiavelli">Machiavelli</a>’s <i><a href="/wiki/Florentine_Histories" title="Florentine Histories">Florentine Histories</a></i>, which he commissioned; and <a href="/wiki/Copernicus" class="mw-redirect" title="Copernicus">Copernicus</a>’ <a href="/wiki/Heliocentrism" title="Heliocentrism">heliocentric idea</a>, which he personally approved in 1533.<sup id="cite_ref-auto3_30-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto3-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-93" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-93">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-auto1_14-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto1-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-auto6_15-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto6-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-auto_16-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> When <a href="/wiki/Johann_Widmanstetter" class="mw-redirect" title="Johann Widmanstetter">Johann Widmanstetter</a> explained the <a href="/wiki/Copernican_heliocentrism" title="Copernican heliocentrism">Copernican system</a> to him, he was so grateful that he gave Widmanstetter a valuable gift.<sup id="cite_ref-Repcheck_94-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Repcheck-94">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup> In 1531 Clement issued rules for the oversight of human cadaver dissection and medical test trials, a sort of primitive code of medical ethics.<sup id="cite_ref-95" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-95">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup> Humanist and author <a href="/wiki/Paolo_Giovio" title="Paolo Giovio">Paolo Giovio</a> was his personal physician.<sup id="cite_ref-96" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-96">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Giulio de’ Medici was a talented musician, and his circle included many well-known artists and thinkers of the Italian <a href="/wiki/High_Renaissance" title="High Renaissance">High Renaissance</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-97" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-97">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup> For example, "in the days before his papacy, the future Clement VII had been close to <a href="/wiki/Leonardo_da_Vinci" title="Leonardo da Vinci">Leonardo da Vinci</a>," with Leonardo gifting him a painting, the <i><a href="/wiki/Madonna_of_the_Carnation" title="Madonna of the Carnation">Madonna of the Carnation</a></i>.<sup id="cite_ref-Strathern_35-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Strathern-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> He was a patron of the satirist <a href="/wiki/Pietro_Aretino" title="Pietro Aretino">Pietro Aretino</a>, who "wrote a series of viciously satirical lampoons supporting the candidacy of Giulio de’ Medici for the papacy."<sup id="cite_ref-98" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-98">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup> As Pope, he appointed author <a href="/wiki/Baldassare_Castiglione" title="Baldassare Castiglione">Baldassare Castiglione</a> as Papal diplomat to <a href="/wiki/Holy_Roman_Emperor_Charles_V" class="mw-redirect" title="Holy Roman Emperor Charles V">Holy Roman Emperor Charles V</a>; and historian <a href="/wiki/Francesco_Guicciardini" title="Francesco Guicciardini">Francesco Guicciardini</a> as governor of the <a href="/wiki/Romagna" title="Romagna">Romagna</a>, the northernmost province of the Papal States.<sup id="cite_ref-99" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-99">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-100" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-100">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="The_Clementine_Style">The Clementine Style</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Clement_VII&amp;action=edit&amp;section=24" title="Edit section: The Clementine Style">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>Italian Renaissance artistic trends from 1523 to 1527 are sometimes called the "Clementine style," and notable for their technical virtuosity.<sup id="cite_ref-101" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-101">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup> In 1527, the Sack of Rome "put a brutal end to an artistic golden age, the Clementine style that had developed in Rome since the coronation of the Medici Pope".<sup id="cite_ref-102" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-102">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Andr%C3%A9_Chastel" title="André Chastel">André Chastel</a> describes the artists who worked in the Clementine style as <a href="/wiki/Parmigianino" title="Parmigianino">Parmigianino</a>, <a href="/wiki/Rosso_Fiorentino" title="Rosso Fiorentino">Rosso Fiorentino</a>, Sebastiano del Piombo, Benvenuto Cellini, <a href="/wiki/Marcantonio_Raimondi" title="Marcantonio Raimondi">Marcantonio Raimondi</a>, and numerous associates of Raphael: <a href="/wiki/Giulio_Romano" title="Giulio Romano">Giulio Romano</a>, <a href="/wiki/Giovanni_da_Udine" title="Giovanni da Udine">Giovanni da Udine</a>; <a href="/wiki/Perino_del_Vaga" title="Perino del Vaga">Perino del Vaga</a>; and <a href="/wiki/Polidoro_da_Caravaggio" title="Polidoro da Caravaggio">Polidoro da Caravaggio</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-auto5_103-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto5-103">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup> During the Sack, several of these artists were either killed, made prisoner, or took part in the fighting.<sup id="cite_ref-auto5_103-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto5-103">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Character">Character</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Clement_VII&amp;action=edit&amp;section=25" title="Edit section: Character">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Giorgio_vasari,_cena_in_casa_di_san_gregorio_magno,_1540,_da_s._giovanni_in_bosco,_04.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/28/Giorgio_vasari%2C_cena_in_casa_di_san_gregorio_magno%2C_1540%2C_da_s._giovanni_in_bosco%2C_04.jpg/220px-Giorgio_vasari%2C_cena_in_casa_di_san_gregorio_magno%2C_1540%2C_da_s._giovanni_in_bosco%2C_04.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="173" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/28/Giorgio_vasari%2C_cena_in_casa_di_san_gregorio_magno%2C_1540%2C_da_s._giovanni_in_bosco%2C_04.jpg/330px-Giorgio_vasari%2C_cena_in_casa_di_san_gregorio_magno%2C_1540%2C_da_s._giovanni_in_bosco%2C_04.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/28/Giorgio_vasari%2C_cena_in_casa_di_san_gregorio_magno%2C_1540%2C_da_s._giovanni_in_bosco%2C_04.jpg/440px-Giorgio_vasari%2C_cena_in_casa_di_san_gregorio_magno%2C_1540%2C_da_s._giovanni_in_bosco%2C_04.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2936" data-file-height="2304" /></a><figcaption>Pope Clement VII as <a href="/wiki/Saint_Gregory_the_Great" class="mw-redirect" title="Saint Gregory the Great">Saint Gregory the Great</a> by <a href="/wiki/Giorgio_Vasari" title="Giorgio Vasari">Giorgio Vasari</a>.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Clement was renowned for his intelligence and counsel, but maligned for his inability to take timely and decisive action. Historian G.F. Young writes, "he spoke with equal knowledge of his subject whether that were philosophy and theology, or mechanics and hydraulic architecture. In all affairs he displayed an extraordinary acuteness; the most perplexing questions were unravelled, the most difficult circumstances penetrated to the very bottom, by his extreme sagacity. No man could debate a point with more address."<sup id="cite_ref-104" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-104">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup> Historian Paul Strathern writes, "his inner life was illuminated by an unwavering faith;" he was also in "surprisingly close contact with the ideals [of Renaissance humanism], and even more surprisingly was deeply sympathetic to them."<sup id="cite_ref-Strathern_35-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Strathern-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> For example, "Clement VII had no difficulty in accepting <a href="/wiki/Copernicus" class="mw-redirect" title="Copernicus">Copernicus</a>’s <a href="/wiki/Heliocentrism" title="Heliocentrism">heliocentric idea</a>, and appeared to see no challenge to his faith in its implications; his Renaissance humanism was open to such progressive theories."<sup id="cite_ref-Strathern_35-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Strathern-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> Of Clement's other qualities, Strathern writes "he had inherited <a href="/wiki/Giuliano_de_Medici" class="mw-redirect" title="Giuliano de Medici">his murdered father</a>’s good looks, though these tended to lapse into a dark scowl rather than a smile. He also inherited something of his great-grandfather <a href="/wiki/Cosimo_de%E2%80%99_Medici" class="mw-redirect" title="Cosimo de’ Medici">Cosimo de’ Medici</a>’s skill with accounts, as well as a strong inclination to his legendary caution, making the new pope hesitant when it came to taking important decisions; and unlike his cousin Leo X, he possessed a deep understanding of art."<sup id="cite_ref-Strathern_35-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Strathern-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Of Clement's limitations, historian <a href="/wiki/Francesco_Guicciardini" title="Francesco Guicciardini">Francesco Guicciardini</a> writes, "although he had a most capable intelligence and marvelous knowledge of world affairs, he lacked the corresponding resolution and execution.... He remained almost always in suspension and ambiguous when he was faced with deciding those things that from afar he had many times foreseen, considered, and almost revealed."<sup id="cite_ref-105" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-105">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup> Strathern writes that Clement was "a man of almost icy self-control, but in him the Medici trait of self-contained caution had deepened into a flaw.... If anything, Clement VII had too much understanding—he could always see both sides of any particular argument. This had made him an excellent close adviser to his cousin Leo X, but hampered his ability to take matters into his own hands."<sup id="cite_ref-Strathern_35-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Strathern-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Encyclopedia" title="Catholic Encyclopedia">Catholic Encyclopedia</a> notes that while his "private life was free from reproach and he had many excellent impulses ... despite good intention, all qualities of heroism and greatness must emphatically be denied him."<sup id="cite_ref-106" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-106">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div style="clear:both;"></div>

<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="References">References</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Clement_VII&amp;action=edit&amp;section=27" title="Edit section: References">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>

<li id="cite_note-Ives-56"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Ives_56-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">For the dates and details of Henry VIII's controversial second marriage, see <link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"><cite id="CITEREFIves2004" class="citation book cs1"><a href="/wiki/Eric_Ives" title="Eric Ives">Ives, Eric William</a> (20 August 2004). <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://archive.org/details/lifedeathofanneb00ives/page/160"><i>The Life and Death of Anne Boleyn: 'The Most Happy'<span></span></i></a>. <a href="/wiki/Malden,_Massachusetts" title="Malden, Massachusetts">Malden, Massachusetts</a>; <a href="/wiki/Oxford" title="Oxford">Oxford</a>; <a href="/wiki/Carlton,_Victoria" title="Carlton, Victoria">Carlton, Victoria</a>: <a href="/wiki/Blackwell_Publishing" class="mw-redirect" title="Blackwell Publishing">Blackwell Publishing</a>. pp.&#160;<a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://archive.org/details/lifedeathofanneb00ives/page/160">160–171</a>. <a href="/wiki/ISBN_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="ISBN (identifier)">ISBN</a>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Special:BookSources/978-0-631-23479-1" title="Special:BookSources/978-0-631-23479-1"><bdi>978-0-631-23479-1</bdi></a>.</cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=book&amp;rft.btitle=The+Life+and+Death+of+Anne+Boleyn%3A+%27The+Most+Happy%27&amp;rft.place=Malden%2C+Massachusetts%3B+Oxford%3B+Carlton%2C+Victoria&amp;rft.pages=160-171&amp;rft.pub=Blackwell+Publishing&amp;rft.date=2004-08-20&amp;rft.isbn=978-0-631-23479-1&amp;rft.aulast=Ives&amp;rft.aufirst=Eric+William&amp;rft_id=https%3A%2F%2Farchive.org%2Fdetails%2Flifedeathofanneb00ives%2Fpage%2F160&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3APope+Clement+VII" class="Z3988"></span></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-57"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-57">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Thomas Cranmer: Churchman and Scholar. By Paul Ayris and David Selwyn. Boydell &amp; Brewer Ltd, 1 January 1999 (pp. 119–121)</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-58"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-58">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Cranmer, in a letter, describes it as a <i>divorce</i>, but it was clearly not a dissolution of a marriage in the modern sense but the annulment of a marriage which was said to be defective on the grounds of affinity—Catherine was his deceased brother's widow.  In his decree, Cranmer uses the words, "...dictum matrimonium..., ut praemittitur, contractum et consummatum, nullum et omnino invalidum fuisse et esse..." <link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"><cite id="CITEREFGilbert_Burnet1825" class="citation book cs1 cs1-prop-long-vol cs1-prop-foreign-lang-source">Gilbert Burnet (1825). <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://books.google.com/books?id=-u83AQAAMAAJ"><i>The History of the Reformation of the Church of England ... in Six Volumes</i></a> (in Latin). Vol.&#160;I, Part II. London: W. Baynes and Son. p.&#160;153.</cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=book&amp;rft.btitle=The+History+of+the+Reformation+of+the+Church+of+England+...+in+Six+Volumes&amp;rft.place=London&amp;rft.pages=153&amp;rft.pub=W.+Baynes+and+Son&amp;rft.date=1825&amp;rft.au=Gilbert+Burnet&amp;rft_id=https%3A%2F%2Fbooks.google.com%2Fbooks%3Fid%3D-u83AQAAMAAJ&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3APope+Clement+VII" class="Z3988"></span></span>
</li>

<li id="cite_note-107"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-107">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Medici does not appear as Bishop either in F. Ughelli, <i>Italia sacra</i> II (ed. N. Colet) (Venice 1717), p. 37; or in Pius Gams, <i>Series episcoporum</i> (1873), p. 676.  Considering the time span, some eight weeks, it is more likely that he was Administrator. On 3 March, the day that Medici resigned, Cardinal Grassis (who had been Bishop of Bologna) was named Administrator of Bologna. </span>
